{{ElectionsTX}}
The '''2026 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas''' will be held on November 3, 2026, to elect the thirty-eight [[U.S. representative]]s from the [[U.S. state|state]] of [[Texas]], one from each of the state's thirty-eight [[Texas's congressional districts|congressional districts]]. The elections will coincide with [[2026 United States House of Representatives elections|other elections]] to the House of Representatives, [[2026 United States Senate elections|elections]] to the [[United States Senate]], and various [[2026 United States elections|state and local elections]]. The primary elections took place on March 3, 2026, and in races where no candidate receives over 50% in a primary, runoff elections took place on May 26.<ref>{{Cite web |date=May 9, 2025 |title=2026 State Primary Election Dates |url=https://www.ncsl.org/elections-and-campaigns/2026-state-primary-election-dates|website= NCSL |access-date=August 5, 2025}}</ref>

== Redistricting ==
{{main|2025 Texas redistricting}}
On July 9, 2025, the [[Government of Texas|Texas state government]] announced that during a planned special session on July 21 of that year, it would consider mid-decade redistricting. This had been pushed privately by the [[White House]] to help Republicans keep control of the House in 2026, and critics have labeled it a [[Gerrymandering|gerrymander]].<ref>{{cite news |title=White House Pushes Texas to Redistrict, Hoping to Blunt Democratic Gains |website=[[The New York Times]] |date=June 9, 2025 |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2025/06/09/us/politics/trump-texas-redistricting.html}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |title=Governor Abbott Announces Special Session Agenda |url=https://gov.texas.gov/news/post/governor-abbott-announces-special-session-agenda-}}</ref>

On August 20, 2025, the [[Texas House of Representatives|Texas House]] passed congressional maps that would target five Democratic-held seats. The vote was 88–52, a party-line vote. The new map changes the territory of Democratic representatives [[Marc Veasey]], [[Vicente Gonzalez (American politician)|Vicente Gonzalez]], [[Lloyd Doggett]], [[Julie Johnson (politician)|Julie Johnson]], and [[Al Green (politician)|Al Green]].<ref>{{cite news |author1=Oren Oppenheim |author2=Monica Madden |author3=Brittany Shepherd |author4=Ivan Pereira |date=August 21, 2025 |title=Texas House passes new GOP-friendly congressional maps |publisher=ABC News |via=MSN |url=https://www.msn.com/en-us/news/politics/texas-house-passes-new-gop-friendly-congressional-maps/ar-AA1KSIbV}}</ref> On August 23, 2025, the Texas Senate passed the map with a vote 18–8. Governor [[Greg Abbott]] has signed the map into law, and therefore will be the active map used in the 2026 House elections in Texas.

On November 18, 2025, a federal court blocked Texas from using its newly drawn congressional map in the 2026 midterms, ruling that the map is likely an unconstitutional "racial gerrymander".<ref name=":3">{{Cite news |last1=Corasaniti |first1=Nick |last2=Goodman |first2=J. David |date=November 18, 2025 |title=Federal Court Blocks Texas' Republican-Friendly Congressional Map |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2025/11/18/us/politics/texas-map-ruling-redistricting.html |access-date=November 18, 2025 |work=The New York Times |language=en-US |issn=0362-4331}}</ref> Three days later on November 21, [[Associate Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States|Supreme Court Justice]] [[Samuel Alito]] granted the request by the state to pause the court's ruling that reverts the election back to using the maps drawn in 2021 until the full [[Supreme Court of the United States]] could make a decision.<ref name="rulingpause">{{Cite news |last=Klibanoff|first=Eleanor|date=November 21, 2025|title=Supreme Court temporarily restores Texas' new congressional map |url=https://www.texastribune.org/2025/11/21/texas-redistricting-supreme-court-temporary-stay/|access-date=November 21, 2025 |work=Texas Tribune|language=en-US}}</ref><ref name="cbsrulingpause">{{Cite news |last=Walsh|first=Joe|date=November 21, 2025|title=Supreme Court halts ruling that tossed out Texas' House maps — for now|url=https://www.cbsnews.com/news/texas-asks-supreme-court-to-block-ruling-tossing-out-new-house-maps/|access-date=November 21, 2025 |work=CBS News|language=en-US}}</ref> On December 4, the Supreme Court ruled that Texas can use the new map in the 2026 midterm elections, striking down the lower court's ruling.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Hurley |first=Lawrence |date=December 4, 2025 |title=Supreme Court allows Texas to use new congressional district map drawn to favor Republicans |url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/supreme-court/supreme-court-allows-texas-use-new-congressional-district-map-drawn-fa-rcna244696 |access-date=December 4, 2025 |website=NBC News |language=en}}</ref>

{{see also|Texas's 1st congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 1st congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 1st congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br/>{{maplink|from=Texas's 1st congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 1st district encompasses much of [[East Texas]], including [[Tyler, Texas|Tyler]], [[Longview, Texas|Longview]], [[Nacogdoches, Texas|Nacogdoches]] and [[Texarkana, Texas|Texarkana]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Nathaniel Moran]], who was re-elected unopposed in 2024. Donald Trump and Ted Cruz won the district in 2024 with 74.3% and 72.5% of the vote, respectively, in 2024.<ref name="HPVT">{{cite web|url=https://www.cookpolitical.com/vote-tracker/2024/house|title=2024 House Vote Tracker|access-date=December 2, 2024 |website=[[The Cook Political Report with Amy Walter|Cook Political Report]]}}</ref>

==== Eliminated in runoff ====
* Dax Alexander, software developer<ref>{{cite news |title=East Texas native Dax Alexander plans to challenge U.S. Rep. ==District 2==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 2nd congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 2

{{see also|Texas's 2nd congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 2nd congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 2nd congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 2nd congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 2nd district encompasses most of the northern and northeastern suburbs of Houston, including [[The Woodlands, Texas|The Woodlands]], [[Spring, Texas|Spring]], [[Kingwood, Texas|Kingwood]], [[New Caney, Texas|New Caney]], [[Humble, Texas|Humble]], and [[Atascocita, Texas|Atascocita]], as well as the [[Willowbrook, Houston|Willowbrook]] area of Houston itself. The incumbent is Republican [[Dan Crenshaw]], who was re-elected with 65.7% of the vote in 2024, will not be on the general election ballot since he was defeated in the primary by State Representative [[Steve Toth]].<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump and Ted Cruz won 60.8% and 58.0% of the vote, respectively, in this district in 2024.

===Republican primary===
====Nominee====
* [[Steve Toth]], [[Texas House of Representatives|state representative]] from the [[Texas's 15th House of Representatives district|15th district]] (2013–2015, 2019–present) and candidate for the 8th Congressional district in [[2016 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 8|2016]]<ref name="Toth">{{cite news |work=[[The Texas Tribune]] |access-date=July 17, 2025 |url=https://www.texastribune.org/2025/07/15/steve-toth-dan-crenshaw-texas-republican-primary-congress/ |date=July 15, 2025 |author=Birenbuam, Gabby |title=State Rep. Steve Toth to challenge Congressman Dan Crenshaw in Republican primary}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====
* [[Dan Crenshaw]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref name="Filing"/>
* Martin Etwop, Christian missionary and candidate for this district in [[2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 2|2022]]<ref>{{cite web |website=Katy Christian Magazine |access-date=September 12, 2025 |url=https://katychristianmagazine.com/2025/05/15/meet-martin-etwop-ugandan-immigrant-freedom-fighter-and-grassroots-conservative-for-texas-2nd-district/ |date=May 15, 2025 |title=Meet Martin Etwop, Ugandan Immigrant, Freedom Fighter, and Grassroots Conservative for Texas' 2nd District}}</ref>
* Nicholas Lee Plumb, retail manager<ref name="Filing"/>

====Withdrawn====
* Jon Bonck, mortgage broker ''(running in the 38th district)''<ref name=third/>
* [[Valentina Gomez]], financial strategist and candidate for [[Missouri Secretary of State|Missouri secretary of state]] in [[2024 Missouri Secretary of State election|2024]] ''(ran in the 31st district)''<ref name="VG2">{{cite news |last1=Bugenhagen |first1=Faith |title='Don't be weak and gay' candidate backtracks on run against Dan Crenshaw |url=https://www.chron.com/politics/article/valentina-gomez-dan-crenshaw-20186797.php |access-date=February 25, 2025 |work=[[Houston Chronicle]] |date=February 25, 2025}}</ref>
* Nick Tran, businessman ''(ran in the 8th district)''<ref name="Filing"/>

====Declined====
* Jameson Ellis, marketing executive and candidate for this district in [[2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 2|2022]] and [[2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 2|2024]]<ref name="Toth"/>

{{see also|Texas's 3rd congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 3rd congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 3rd congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 3rd congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 3rd district encompasses much of [[Collin County, Texas|Collin County]] and [[Hunt County, Texas|Hunt County]] in the [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex|Dallas–Fort Worth Metroplex]], including eastern [[Plano, Texas|Plano]], [[McKinney, Texas|McKinney]], [[Allen, Texas|Allen]], [[Wylie, Texas|Wylie]] and [[Greenville, Texas|Greenville]], as well as much of the [[Interstate 30 in Texas|I-30]] corridor to the east including [[Sulphur Springs, Texas|Sulphur Springs]] and [[Mount Pleasant, Texas|Mount Pleasant]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Keith Self]], who was re-elected with 62.5% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump and Ted Cruz each respectively won 60.3% and 57.7% of the vote here in 2024.

====Nominee====
* [[Keith Self]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref>{{cite news |last1=Morales |first1=R. ==District 4==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 4th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 4

{{see also|Texas's 4th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 4th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 4th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 4th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 4th district encompasses most of the counties in the eastern part of the [[Texoma]] region along the [[Red River of the South|Red River]], including the communities of [[Sherman, Texas|Sherman]] and [[Paris, Texas|Paris]], as well as some sections of the suburban and exurban [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex|DFW Metroplex]] including [[Frisco, Texas|Frisco]], most of [[Plano, Texas|Plano]] and the Collin County portion of [[Dallas]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Pat Fallon]], who was re-elected with 68.4% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump and Ted Cruz each respectively won 61.2% and 59.0% of the vote here in 2024.

===Republican primary===
====Nominee====
* [[Pat Fallon]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref name="Filing"/>

====Eliminated in primary====
* Don Horn, farmer and candidate for this seat in [[2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 4|2024]]<ref name="Filing"/>

==District 5==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 5th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 5
| previous_year = 2024

| party1 = Republican Party (United States)

{{see also|Texas's 5th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 5th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 5th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 5th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 5th district encompasses the southeastern parts of the [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex|Dallas–Fort Worth Metroplex]], including [[Mesquite, Texas|Mesquite]], [[Terrell, Texas|Terrell]], [[Palestine, Texas|Palestine]], [[Athens, Texas|Athens]], [[Canton, Texas|Canton]], [[Kaufman, Texas|Kaufman]], the southern halves of [[Garland, Texas|Garland]] and [[Rowlett, Texas|Rowlett]], and the [[Lakewood, Dallas|Lakewood]] and [[Lake Highlands]] portions of [[Dallas]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Lance Gooden]], who was re-elected with 64.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump and Ted Cruz each respectively won 60.1% and 56.9% of the vote in this district in 2024.

====Nominee====
* [[Lance Gooden]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref name="Filing"/>

==District 6==

{{see also|Texas's 6th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 6th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 6th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 6th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 6th district encompasses most of the southern parts of the [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex|Dallas–Fort Worth Metroplex]], including most or all of the suburbs of [[Midlothian, Texas|Midlothian]], [[Mansfield, Texas|Mansfield]], [[Burleson, Texas|Burleson]], [[Waxahachie, Texas|Waxahachie]] and [[Corsicana, Texas|Corsicana]], as well as most of the west side of [[Arlington, Texas|Arlington]] and south and central [[Irving, Texas|Irving]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Jake Ellzey]], who was re-elected with 66.4% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump and Ted Cruz won 60.4% and 57.4%, respectively, in this district in 2024.

====Nominee====

* Brian Stahl, [[Covington, Texas|Covington]] city councilman<ref name="Filing"/>

==District 7==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 7th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 7
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 7
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Lizzie Fletcher portrait (118th Congress).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Lizzie Fletcher]]
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Alexander Hale
| party2 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 =

{{see also|Texas's 7th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 7th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 7th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 7th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 7th district encompasses a diverse southwestern stretch of the [[Greater Houston]] area across Harris and Fort Bend counties, including [[Uptown Houston|the Galleria area]] of Houston and the inner West Loop neighborhoods of [[Montrose, Houston|Montrose]], [[Meyerland, Houston|Meyerland]], [[Rice Military, Houston|Rice Military]], [[Houston Heights|the Heights]] and [[Greenway Plaza]], along with such diverse southwest Houston neighborhoods as [[Westchase, Houston|Westchase]], [[Sharpstown, Houston|Sharpstown]], [[Gulfton, Houston|Gulfton]] and [[Alief, Houston|Alief]], and largely Asian and Hispanic portions of western Sugar Land and Mission Bend in Fort Bend County. The incumbent is Democrat [[Lizzie Fletcher]], who was re-elected with 61.2% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> The diverse district gave 60.3% to Kamala Harris and 63.1% to Colin Allred in 2024.

==District 8==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 8th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 8
| previous_year = 2024

| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Jessica Hart Steinmann.png
| nominee1 = Jessica Steinmann
| party1 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Laura Jones
| party2 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size =

| before_election = [[Morgan Luttrell]]

{{see also|Texas's 8th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 8th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 8th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 8th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The 8th district includes most of the northern and northwestern exurbs of [[Houston, Texas|Houston]], including [[Conroe, Texas|Conroe]], part of [[Huntsville, Texas|Huntsville]], [[Willis, Texas|Willis]], [[Magnolia, Texas|Magnolia]], [[Brookshire, Texas|Brookshire]] and [[Hempstead, Texas|Hempstead]], along with parts of west Houston including Briar Forest, the western end of the [[Houston Energy Corridor|Energy Corridor]] and most of the Bear Creek and [[Addicks, Houston|Addicks]] areas in west Harris County. The incumbent is Republican [[Morgan Luttrell]], who was elected with 68.2% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Luttrell is not seeking reelection in the heavily Republican district, which gave Donald Trump 63.2% and Ted Cruz 60.1% of the vote in 2024 and is a plurality White district with a 31.3% Hispanic voting age population.<ref name=":1" />

==District 9==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 9th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 9

{{see also|Texas's 9th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 9th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 9th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 9th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 9th district, which previously encompassed southern portions of [[Houston, Texas|Houston]] and such suburbs as [[Missouri City, Texas|Missouri City]] and was represented by incumbent Democrat [[Al Green (politician)|Al Green]], has been relocated to the eastern portions of the Houston area as a result of redistricting; Green ran for reelection in the newly redrawn 18th district.<ref name="HPVT" />

The new district, which has a 58.6% Hispanic voting age population, covers such east and southeast Houston neighborhoods as [[Denver Harbor, Houston|Denver Harbor]], [[Magnolia Park, Houston|Magnolia Park]], [[Park Place, Houston|Park Place]] and Edgebrook, as well as most of the suburbs of [[Pasadena, Texas|Pasadena]], [[Baytown, Texas|Baytown]], [[Deer Park, Texas|Deer Park]], [[La Porte, Texas|La Porte]], [[Galena Park, Texas|Galena Park]], [[Channelview, Texas|Channelview]] and [[Crosby, Texas|Crosby]], and exurban [[Liberty County, Texas|Liberty County]] including [[Cleveland, Texas|Cleveland]], [[Liberty, Texas|Liberty]] and [[Dayton, Texas|Dayton]]. Donald Trump carried the district in all three of his elections - a 49.8% plurality in 2016, 53.7% in 2020, and 59.5% in 2024, and the district also gave Ted Cruz 54.4% of the vote in 2024.

==District 10==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 10th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 10

{{see also|Texas's 10th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 10th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 10th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 10th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The incumbent is Republican [[Michael McCaul]], who was re-elected in 2024 with 63.6% of the vote. McCaul is not seeking reelection. The district was redrawn during Texas's [[2025 Texas redistricting|2025 mid-decade congressional redistricting,]] which gave 60.5% of the vote to Donald Trump and 58.4% to Ted Cruz in 2024.<ref name="HPVT" />

The new 10th district stretches from downtown and western [[Austin, Texas|Austin]] (including [[Lake Travis]]) through the [[Bryan–College Station]] area to a rural stretch of east-central Texas between Houston, Dallas and Tyler, including [[Crockett, Texas|Crockett]], [[Livingston, Texas|Livingston]] and [[Madisonville, Texas|Madisonville]].

==District 11==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 11th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 11
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 11
| next_year = 2028

| nominee1 = [[August Pfluger]]
| party1 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Claire Reynolds
| party2 = Democratic Party (United States)

{{see also|Texas's 11th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 11th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 11th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 11th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 11th district is based in midwestern Texas, including [[Midland, Texas|Midland]], [[Odessa, Texas|Odessa]], [[San Angelo]] and [[Brownwood, Texas|Brownwood]], and also includes a thin stretch of the Austin area along the Travis and Williamson county lines including [[Pflugerville, Texas|Pflugerville]] and [[Horseshoe Bay, Texas|Horseshoe Bay]]. The incumbent is Republican [[August Pfluger]], who was re-elected unopposed in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> The majority White district has a voting age population that is 35.3% Hispanic, and in 2024 gave Donald Trump 66.5% of the vote and Ted Cruz 64%.

==District 12==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 12th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 12
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 12
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Representative Craig Goldman Official Portrait (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Craig Goldman]]
| party1 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Angela Rodriguez Prilliman
| party2 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size = 
| map_caption = 
| title = U.S. Representative
| before_election = [[Craig Goldman]]
| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election =

{{see also|Texas's 12th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 12th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 12th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 12th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 12th district is in the western part of the [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex]], and takes in most of [[Parker County, Texas|Parker County]] and western [[Tarrant County, Texas|Tarrant County]], including most of the western half of [[Fort Worth, Texas|Fort Worth]] and such inner suburbs as [[Benbrook, Texas|Benbrook]], [[Saginaw, Texas|Saginaw]], and [[Haltom City, Texas|Haltom City]], as well as [[Weatherford, Texas|Weatherford]] in Parker County. The incumbent is Republican [[Craig Goldman]], who was elected with 63.5% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump won 61.3% and Ted Cruz 57.9% in 2024.

==District 13==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 13th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential

{{see also|Texas's 13th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 13th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 13th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 13th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 13th district encompasses most of the [[Texas Panhandle]] and the western part of the [[Texoma]] region, containing the cities of [[Amarillo, Texas|Amarillo]] and [[Wichita Falls, Texas|Wichita Falls]], as well as the college town of [[Denton, Texas|Denton]] in Denton County. The incumbent is Republican [[Ronny Jackson]], who was re-elected unopposed in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump won 72.5% of the vote and Ted Cruz 70.3% in this district.

==District 14==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 14th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 14

{{see also|Texas's 14th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 14th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 14th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 14th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 14th district remains anchored in [[Galveston County, Texas|Galveston County]] in the southeast corner of [[Greater Houston]], including [[Galveston, Texas|Galveston]], [[League City, Texas|League City]], [[Friendswood, Texas|Friendswood]] and [[Texas City, Texas|Texas City]], and also now extends westward to [[Manvel, Texas|Manvel]] and [[Alvin, Texas|Alvin]] in north central Brazoria County and southern [[Missouri City, Texas|Missouri City]] in Fort Bend County, as well east across [[Bolivar Peninsula, Texas|Bolivar Peninsula]] to [[Port Arthur, Texas|Port Arthur]] and [[Orange, Texas|Orange]] in the [[Beaumont–Port Arthur metropolitan area|Golden Triangle]] area. The incumbent is Republican [[Randy Weber]], who was re-elected with 68.7% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Donald Trump won 61.5 percent of the vote and Ted Cruz 58.6 in this district in 2024.

==District 15==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 15th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 15
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 15
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = File:MDLC Official Portrait 2025 (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Monica De La Cruz]]
| party1 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = Bobby23 (cropped).jpg
| nominee2 = [[Bobby Pulido]]
| party2 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size = 
| map_caption = 
| title = U.S. Representative
| before_election = [[Monica De La Cruz]]
| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election = 
| after_party = 
}}
{{see also|Texas's 15th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 15th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 15th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 15th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 15th district stretches from [[Hidalgo County, Texas|Hidalgo County]] in the [[Rio Grande Valley (Texas)|Rio Grande Valley]] (including Edinburg and Weslaco), and now extends northeasterly into several rural counties between Corpus Christi, San Antonio and Victoria, including such communities as [[Falfurrias, Texas|Falfurrias]], [[Alice, Texas|Alice]], [[Sinton, Texas|Sinton]], [[Beeville, Texas|Beeville]], [[Cuero, Texas|Cuero]] and [[Gonzales, Texas|Gonzales]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Monica De La Cruz]], who was re-elected with 57.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

In 2024, Donald Trump won 58.5% in this overwhelmingly Hispanic district, which gave Ted Cruz 53.5% in the same election (six years after Cruz lost to Beto O'Rourke, who won 55.4% in the 2018 election for the same Senate seat). Hillary Clinton won the district with 55% in 2016 before it flipped to Trump (who won 50.7%) in 2020.

==District 16==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 16th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 16
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 16
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Veronica Escobar official portrait, 116th Congress (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Veronica Escobar]]
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Adam Bauman
| party2 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size =

{{see also|Texas's 16th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 16th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 16th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 16th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 16th district is entirely within [[El Paso County, Texas|El Paso County]], taking in [[El Paso, Texas|El Paso]] and such surrounding suburbs as [[Socorro, Texas|Socorro]], [[Horizon City, Texas|Horizon City]], and [[Anthony, Texas|Anthony]]. The incumbent is Democrat [[Veronica Escobar]], who was re-elected with 59.5% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> In 2024, Kamala Harris won 57.4% of the vote in this heavily Hispanic district, where Colin Allred also won with 58.4% of the vote.

==District 17==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 17th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 17
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 17
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Pete Sessions, official portrait, 117th Congress (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Pete Sessions]]
| party1 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Casey Shepard
| party2 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size =

| title = U.S. Representative
| before_election = [[Pete Sessions]]
| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election =

{{see also|Texas's 17th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 17th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 17th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 17th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 17th district is anchored in [[Waco, Texas|Waco]] and its surrounding [[Waco metropolitan area|metropolitan area]], with a small sliver of the district extending into the east side of Temple and a southern sliver of Williamson County, with the Austin suburb of [[Cedar Park, Texas|Cedar Park]] connected to the rest of the district via a small sliver of [[Round Rock, Texas|Round Rock]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Pete Sessions]], who was re-elected with 66.4% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> The new district gave 60% of the vote to Donald Trump and 57.5% to Ted Cruz in 2024.

==District 18==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 18th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2025–26 Texas's 18th congressional district special election
| previous_year = 2025–26 (special)
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 18
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Christian Menefee, official portrait (119th Congress) (2) (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Christian Menefee]]
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Ronald Whitfield
| party2 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size = 
| map_caption =

| before_election = [[Al Green (politician)|Al Green]] <br />[[Christian Menefee]]
| before_party = Democratic Party (United States)

{{see also|Texas's 18th congressional district}} {{see also|2025–26 Texas's 18th congressional district special election}}
[[File:Texas's 18th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 18th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 18th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 18th district has two incumbents: Democrat [[Al Green (politician)|Al Green]], who was re-elected unopposed in 2024 for the 9th district, and [[Christian Menefee]] who succeeded [[Sylvester Turner]], who died unexpectedly in March 2025, in a special election runoff held in [[2025–26 Texas's 18th congressional district special election|January 2026]].<ref name="HPVT" />

The new district, which has a voting age population that is 45% Black and 32.2% Hispanic, includes the [[Downtown Houston|Downtown]], [[East Downtown Houston|EaDo]], [[Midtown, Houston|Midtown]], [[Third Ward, Houston|Third Ward]] and [[Fifth Ward, Houston|Fifth Ward]] portions of Houston as well as the [[Texas Medical Center]], the [[Houston Museum District|Museum District]] and [[NRG Stadium]], and extends northeast to [[Settegast, Houston|Settegast]] and [[Fall Creek, Texas|Fall Creek]] in northeast Houston, [[Sunnyside, Houston|Sunnyside]] and [[Brays Oaks, Houston|Brays Oaks]] in south and southwest Houston, and northern [[Missouri City, Texas|Missouri City]], [[Stafford, Texas|Stafford]] and [[Fresno, Texas|Fresno]] in Fort Bend County. In 2024, the district gave Kamala Harris 76.7% of the vote and 78.5% to Colin Allred.

==District 19==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 19th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 19

{{see also|Texas's 19th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 19th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 19th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 19th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 19th district, then as now, encompasses much of [[West Texas]] including [[Lubbock, Texas|Lubbock]] and [[Abilene, Texas|Abilene]] along with [[Big Spring, Texas|Big Spring]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Jodey Arrington]], who was re-elected with 80.7% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Arrington is not seeking reelection to a sixth term in the heavily Republican district, which gave 75.3% of the vote to Donald Trump and 73% to Ted Cruz in 2024, and is a majority White district with a voting age population that is 34.7% Hispanic.

====Nominee====
* Tom Sell, businessman<ref>{{cite web |author=KCBD Staff |title=Tom Sell announces candidacy for District 19 representative |url=https://www.kcbd.com/2025/11/13/tom-sell-announces-candidacy-district-19-representative/ |access-date=November 14, 2025 |work=[[KCBD]] |date=November 13, 2025}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====
* James Barbee, business owner<ref name="Filing"/>
* Jason Corley, [[Lubbock County, Texas|Lubbock County]] commissioner (2019–present) and candidate for this district in [[2016 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 19|2016]]<ref>{{cite web |last1=King |first1=Landon |title=County Commissioner Jason Corley Steps Into Race For Texas 19th District |url=https://etsn.fm/ixp/190/p/lubbock-commissioner-corley-campaign/ |access-date=December 2, 2025 |work=ETSN.fm |date=December 2, 2025}}</ref>
* Donald May, surgeon and candidate for this district in [[2003 Texas's 19th congressional district special election|2003]], [[2014 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 19|2014]], and [[2016 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 19|2016]]<ref name="Filing"/>
* Matt Smith, roofing company owner<ref>{{cite web |title=Fat Matt Roofing founder Matt Smith launches campaign for Congress |url=https://www.bigcountryhomepage.com/news/fat-matt-roofing-founder-matt-smith-launches-campaign-for-congress/ |access-date=December 6, 2025 |website=BigCountryHomepage.com |date=December 5, 2025}}</ref>

==District 20==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 20th congressional district election
| country = Texas

{{see also|Texas's 20th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 20th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 20th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 20th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 20th district encompasses downtown [[San Antonio]] and extends eastward to its historically Black east side and the community of [[Kirby, Texas|Kirby]], as well as westward to [[Leon Valley, Texas|Leon Valley]] and several neighborhoods north of [[Lackland Air Force Base|Lackland AFB]]. The incumbent is Democrat [[Joaquin Castro]], who was re-elected unopposed in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Kamala Harris won the two-thirds Hispanic district with 63.5% of the vote and Colin Allred 66.6% in 2024.

==District 21==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 21st congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 21
| previous_year = 2024

| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Mark Teixeira basepaths 2011 (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Mark Teixeira]]
| party1 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Kristin Hook
| party2 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size =

| title = U.S. Representative
| before_election = [[Chip Roy]]
| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election =

{{see also|Texas's 21st congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 21st congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 21st congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 21st congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 21st district takes in the [[Texas Hill Country]], including [[Fredericksburg, Texas|Fredericksburg]], [[Boerne, Texas|Boerne]], [[Kerrville, Texas|Kerrville]] and [[Bandera, Texas|Bandera]], along with Comal County including [[New Braunfels, Texas|New Braunfels]] and most of Hays County including [[San Marcos, Texas|San Marcos]], [[Wimberley, Texas|Wimberley]] and [[Dripping Springs, Texas|Dripping Springs]], as well as most of northwest [[San Antonio]] along with [[Alamo Heights, Texas|Alamo Heights]], [[Castle Hills, Texas|Castle Hills]], the eastern half of [[Stone Oak, San Antonio|Stone Oak]] and [[Fort Sam Houston]] in Bexar County. The incumbent is Republican [[Chip Roy]], who was elected with 61.9% of the vote in 2024 and [[2026 Texas Attorney General election|ran for Texas Attorney General in 2026]], but lost in the primary.<ref name="HPVT"/> A Republican-held district since 1978, Donald Trump won 60.3% of the vote in this largely exurban district, which also gave Ted Cruz 57.7%, both in 2024.

===Republican primary===
====Nominee====
* [[Mark Teixeira]], former [[Major League Baseball|MLB]] player<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.houstonchronicle.com/sports/astros/article/mark-teixeira-congress-politics-donald-trump-texas-21019730.php|title=Touting support for Donald Trump, ex-Rangers, Yankees star Mark Teixeira vying for Congress in Texas|last=Young|first=Matt|date=August 28, 2025|access-date=August 28, 2025|website=[[Houston Chronicle]]}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====
* Daniel Betts, attorney and nominee for [[Travis County, Texas|Travis County]] [[district attorney]] in 2024<ref name="Filing"/>

==District 22==
{{Infobox election

{{see also|Texas's 22nd congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 22nd congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 22nd congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 22nd congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 22nd district encompasses the southwest suburban corner of the [[Greater Houston]] metropolitan area across Harris, Fort Bend and Brazoria counties, including the southern [[Houston]] suburbs of [[Sugar Land, Texas|Sugar Land]], [[Rosenberg, Texas|Rosenberg]], [[Lake Jackson, Texas|Lake Jackson]] and [[Angleton, Texas|Angleton]], as well as the [[Katy, Texas|Katy]] and [[Fulshear, Texas|Fulshear]] areas in both Harris and Fort Bend counties. The incumbent is Republican [[Troy Nehls]], who is not seeking reelection and was re-elected with 62.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> That same year, Donald Trump won 59.9% and Ted Cruz 56.9% of the vote in the district, which is diverse with double-digit populations of White, Hispanic, Asian and Black residents (both voting age and overall).

====Nominee====
* Trever Nehls, former [[Fort Bend County]] [[Constable (Texas)|constable]] from the 4th precinct (2013–2020) and brother of [[Troy Nehls]]<ref>{{cite web|access-date=2026-01-29|title=Trump endorses identical twin of retiring Rep. Troy Nehls to fill his seat|url=https://www.politico.com/live-updates/2025/12/04/congress/trump-endorses-trever-nehls-00677128|date=4 December 2025|website=POLITICO}}</ref>

==District 23==
{{Infobox election

{{see also|Texas's 23rd congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 23rd congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 23rd congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 23rd congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 23rd district covers southwestern Texas, including the [[Big Bend (Texas)|Big Bend]], and stretches eastward through [[Del Rio, Texas|Del Rio]] and [[Uvalde, Texas|Uvalde]] to the northern [[San Antonio]] suburbs including the west side of [[Stone Oak, San Antonio|Stone Oak]], [[Shavano Park, Texas|Shavano Park]] and [[Camp Bullis]] with a small finger extending eastward to [[Lackland Air Force Base|Lackland AFB]] in southwest San Antonio, and also westward to the eastern fringes of the [[El Paso, Texas|El Paso]] suburbs.  The majority Hispanic district gave Donald Trump and Ted Cruz 56.8% and 52.9% of the vote, respectively, in 2024.

The incumbent is Republican [[Tony Gonzales]], who was re-elected with 62.3% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT" /> Republican leaders called on Gonzales to end his re-election campaign after he admitted to having a sexual relationship with a former staff member who later died by suicide, following his earlier denial of the allegations.<ref>{{Cite web |date=March 5, 2026 |title=Republican leaders call on Rep. Tony Gonzales to end his re-election campaign |url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/congress/republican-leaders-call-rep-tony-gonzales-end-reelection-campaign-rcna261960 |access-date=March 5, 2026 |website=NBC News}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=March 4, 2026 |title=Tony Gonzales admits sexual relationship with former staff member who killed herself |url=https://www.politico.com/live-updates/2026/03/04/congress/tony-gonzales-admits-sexual-relationship-with-ex-staff-member-00813660 |access-date=March 5, 2026 |website=Politico}}</ref> Gonzales ended his campaign on March 5,<ref>{{Cite web |date=2026-03-05 |title=Rep. Tony Gonzales drops out of House runoff race after admitting affair with aide - CBS News |url=https://www.cbsnews.com/news/tony-gonzales-drops-out-of-house-runoff-race-after-admitting-affair-with-aide/ |access-date=2026-03-06 |website=www.cbsnews.com |language=en-US}}</ref> leading to the cancellation of the runoff and making challenger Brandon Herrera the Republican nominee.

Gonzales later resigned on April 13 after he was expected to face an expulsion vote alongside Democratic representative [[Eric Swalwell]], who was accused of sexual assault.<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Hill|first1=Meredith Lee|last2=Rogerson|first2=Riley|date=2026-04-13|title=Tony Gonzales eyed for expulsion vote after Swalwell resignation|url=https://www.politico.com/live-updates/2026/04/13/congress/tony-gonzales-expulsion-00870107|access-date=2026-04-14|website=POLITICO|language=en}}</ref> Swalwell similarly had announced his resignation on April 13, hours before Gonzales' announcement.<ref>{{Cite news |last1=Alfaro|first1=Mariana|last2=Wang|first2=Amy B|date=2026-04-13|title=Reps. Swalwell, Gonzales say they'll leave Congress ahead of possible expulsion votes|url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/2026/04/13/eric-swalwell-resigns-congress/|access-date=2026-04-14|newspaper=The Washington Post|language=en-US|issn=0190-8286}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=2026-04-14|title=Reps. Tony Gonzales and Eric Swalwell officially resign amid misconduct claims|url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/congress/reps-tony-gonzales-eric-swalwell-officially-resign-misconduct-claims-rcna331765|access-date=2026-04-14|website=[[NBC News]]|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260414204302/https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/congress/reps-tony-gonzales-eric-swalwell-officially-resign-misconduct-claims-rcna331765|archive-date=2026-04-14|url-status=live}}</ref> Despite multiple other vacancies which occurred around the same time having been filled by August, governor Greg Abbott never called an emergency special election to fill this seat, leaving it vacant until January 2027.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Wallace |first=Jeremy |date=2026-08-26 |title=Abbott leaves nearly a million Texans without a vote in Congress |url=https://www.houstonchronicle.com/politics/texas-take/article/abbott-texans-congress-tony-gonzales-22403054.php |access-date=2026-08-26 |website=Houston Chronicle |language=en}}</ref>

==District 24==
{{Infobox election

{{see also|Texas's 24th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 24th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 24th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 24th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 24th district, centered on [[Dallas Fort Worth International Airport]] in the heart of the [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex]], encompasses the suburbs north of [[Fort Worth]] and [[Dallas]], including [[Grapevine, Texas|Grapevine]], [[Bedford, Texas|Bedford]], [[North Richland Hills, Texas|North Richland Hills]] and [[Southlake, Texas|Southlake]] in northeast Tarrant County, and the wealthy [[Park Cities, Texas|Park Cities]] north of downtown Dallas, as well as the neighboring [[Knox Park, Dallas|Knox Park]] and [[Lower Greenville, Dallas|Lower Greenville]] neighborhoods and most of [[north Dallas]] (including [[Preston Hollow, Dallas|Preston Hollow]]) in Dallas itself and the Dallas County suburbs of [[Farmers Branch, Texas|Farmers Branch]] and [[Coppell, Texas|Coppell]].

Due to redistricting, the district has two incumbents, Republican [[Beth Van Duyne]], who was re-elected with 60.3% of the vote in 2024, and Democrat [[Julie Johnson (politician)|Julie Johnson]], who was elected with 61.9% of the vote in 2024 from the old 32nd District.<ref name="HPVT" /> Donald Trump won 57.1% of the vote in this affluent district, which also gave Ted Cruz 54.6% of the vote that same year against Democrat [[Colin Allred]], whom Johnson succeeded in Congress. Johnson has since decided to seek reelection in the newly redrawn 33rd district (see below).

==District 25==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 25th congressional district election
| country = Texas

{{see also|Texas's 25th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 25th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 25th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 25th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 25th district runs from northern [[Arlington, Texas|Arlington]] and southern and eastern [[Fort Worth, Texas|Fort Worth]] in Tarrant County, whose portion is the only portion of the district considered even remotely competitive (and in fact, favorable) to Democrats, out to several heavily Republican exurban and rural areas south and west of Fort Worth and just east of Abilene, including [[Cleburne, Texas|Cleburne]], [[Granbury, Texas|Granbury]], [[Willow Park, Texas|Willow Park]], [[Mineral Wells, Texas|Mineral Wells]], [[Stephenville, Texas|Stephenville]], [[Jacksboro, Texas|Jacksboro]] and [[Eastland, Texas|Eastland]].

Due to redistricting, the district has two incumbents, Republican [[Roger Williams (Texas politician)|Roger Williams]], who was re-elected unopposed in 2024, and Democrat [[Marc Veasey]], who was re-elected with 68.7% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT" /> Veasey, the incumbent from the old 33rd district (see below) decided to not seek reelection, instead pursuing a short-lived bid for Tarrant County judge before dropping out of that race. Donald Trump won 61.4% of the vote in this district in 2024, which also saw Ted Cruz win 58.4% of the vote.

==District 26==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 26th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 26

{{see also|Texas's 26th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 26th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 26th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 26th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 26th district is based in the northwestern corner of the [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex]], centering on southern and eastern [[Denton County, Texas|Denton County]] (including the county's share of [[Carrollton, Texas|Carrollton]] along with all of [[Lewisville, Texas|Lewisville]], [[Flower Mound, Texas|Flower Mound]] and [[Little Elm, Texas|Little Elm]]) and including [[Cooke County, Texas|Cooke County]] ([[Gainesville, Texas|Gainesville]]) and the southern two-thirds of [[Wise County, Texas|Wise County]] including [[Decatur, Texas|Decatur]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Brandon Gill]], who was elected with 62.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> That same year, the district gave 61.2% of the vote to Donald Trump and 58.4% to Ted Cruz.

[[File:Texas's 27th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 27th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 27th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 27th district stretches across the [[Texas Coastal Bend|Coastal Bend]], from downtown [[Corpus Christi, Texas|Corpus Christi]] and [[Port Aransas, Texas|Port Aransas]] in the south, along with [[Victoria, Texas|Victoria]] and the rural fringes of the Greater Houston area including [[Brenham, Texas|Brenham]], [[Bay City, Texas|Bay City]] and [[Sealy, Texas|Sealy]], extending westward to [[La Grange, Texas|La Grange]] along with the southern and eastern suburbs of Austin including [[Bastrop, Texas|Bastrop]], [[Kyle, Texas|Kyle]] and [[Lockhart, Texas|Lockhart]] along with an eastern sliver of Travis County (including the [[Circuit of the Americas]]).

The incumbent is Republican [[Michael Cloud]], who was re-elected with 66.0% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT" /> A plurality White district whose voting age population is more than 40% Hispanic, Donald Trump won 60% of the vote and Ted Cruz 57.1% in 2024.

==District 28==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 28th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 28
| previous_year = 2024

| next_year = 2028
| image1 = File:Henry Cuellar, official portrait, 115th congress (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Henry Cuellar]]
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 =

{{See also|Texas's 28th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 28th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 28th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 28th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 28th district is based in the [[Laredo, Texas|Laredo]] area and stretches south to [[McAllen, Texas|McAllen]] in the [[Rio Grande Valley (Texas)|Rio Grande Valley]] and north to [[Atascosa County, Texas|Atascosa County]] in the San Antonio area. The incumbent is Democrat [[Henry Cuellar]], who was re-elected with 52.8% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

Historically a heavily Democratic district, Donald Trump won 54.8% of the vote in this overwhelmingly Hispanic district in 2024, which also saw Ted Cruz win a plurality of 48.8% (and a vote margin of only 228 votes) that same year; the district previously gave Democrats Joe Biden 54.3% in 2020 and Hillary Clinton 66.4% in 2016. In U.S. Senate races, John Cornyn lost the district twice in 2014 and 2020 for his seat, as did Cruz in his seat in 2018 against Beto O'Rourke, who won 65.8% of the vote that year.

==District 29==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 29th congressional district election
| country = Texas

{{see also|Texas's 29th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 29th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 29th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 29th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 29th district encompasses much of north [[Houston, Texas|Houston]], taking in the heavily Latino [[Lindale Park, Houston|Lindale Park]] and [[Northline, Houston|Northline]] areas along with historically Black [[Acres Homes, Houston|Acres Homes]] and [[Independence Heights, Houston|Independence Heights]], as well as the [[Garden Oaks, Houston|Garden Oaks]], [[Oak Forest, Houston|Oak Forest]] and [[Fairbanks, Houston|Fairbanks]] areas of northwest Houston, and the [[Aldine, Texas|Aldine]] and [[Greenspoint, Houston|Greenspoint]] areas of far north Houston including [[George Bush Intercontinental Airport]]. The incumbent is Democrat [[Sylvia Garcia]], who was re-elected with 65.2% of the vote in 2024 in the majority Hispanic district, which was won by Kamala Harris (64.5%) and Colin Allred (67.6%) that same year.<ref name="HPVT"/>

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====
* [[Sylvia Garcia]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref name="Sylvia">{{cite news |last1=Wallace |first1=Jeremy |title=Houston's only Hispanic member in U.S. House could have fight to remain in office |url=https://www.houstonchronicle.com/politics/texas-take/article/houston-s-sylvia-garcia-faces-real-reelection-21065474.php |access-date=September 25, 2025 |work=[[Houston Chronicle]] |date=September 25, 2025}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====
* [[Jarvis Johnson]], former [[Texas House of Representatives|state representative]] from the [[Texas's 139th House of Representatives district|139th district]] (2016–2025), candidate for this district in [[2010 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 18|2010]], candidate for [[Texas Senate, District 15|Texas's 15th Senate district]] in the 2024 [[2024 United States state legislative elections#Texas 2|special]] and [[2024 Texas Senate election|regular]] elections, and candidate for the 18th district in [[2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#Replacement nominee selection|2024]]<ref name="Sylvia"/>
* Robert Slater, business owner and candidate for the 18th district in [[2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 18|2024]] and [[2025–26 Texas's 18th congressional district special election|2025]]<ref name="Filing"/>

==District 30==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 30th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 30

| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)

| percentage1 =

| party2 = Republican Party (United States)

{{see also|Texas's 30th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 30th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 30th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 30th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 30th district is anchored in the southern portions of [[Dallas]] and encompasses [[South Dallas]] and [[Fair Park]], stretching southward to such diverse south Dallas County suburbs as [[Lancaster, Texas|Lancaster]], [[Duncanville, Texas|Duncanville]], [[DeSoto, Texas|DeSoto]], [[Wilmer, Texas|Wilmer]], [[Cedar Hill, Texas|Cedar Hill]] and southern [[Grand Prairie, Texas|Grand Prairie]]. Prior to redistricting, the incumbent was Democrat [[Jasmine Crockett]]; however, Crockett was drawn out of the 30th district and into the 33rd and ultimately decided to run for the U.S. Senate. Kamala Harris won 72.7% of the vote in the district in 2024, as did Dallas native Colin Allred who won 75.1% in his losing Senate bid against Ted Cruz.

==District 31==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 31st congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes

{{see also|Texas's 31st congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 31st congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 31st congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 31st congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 31st district is anchored in the northern exurbs of [[Austin, Texas|Austin]] (including [[Georgetown, Texas|Georgetown]] and [[Burnet, Texas|Burnet]]) and stretches northward to [[Killeen, Texas|Killeen]] and most of [[Temple, Texas|Temple]] along with [[Fort Hood]], going as far north as [[Hamilton, Texas|Hamilton]]. The incumbent is Republican [[John Carter (Texas politician)|John Carter]], who was re-elected with 64.5% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> That same year, Donald Trump won 60.1% and Ted Cruz 57.6% of the vote, respectively.

==District 32==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 32nd congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 32
| previous_year = 2024

| next_year = 2028
| image1 = 3x4.svg
| nominee1 = Dan Barrios
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = Joint Base Commander visits the US Air Force Honor Guard - Jace Yarbrough (cropped).jpg
| nominee2 = Jace Yarbrough
| party2 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size = 
| map_caption = 
| title = U.S. Representative
| before_election = ''None''<br />(New seat)
| after_election = 
| after_party =

{{see also|Texas's 32nd congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 32nd congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 32nd congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 32nd congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 32nd district covers several suburban areas directly north and northeast of [[Dallas]], including most of the [[Dallas County, Texas|Dallas County]] portion of [[Carrollton, Texas|Carrollton]] and [[Addison, Texas|Addison]] along with all of [[Richardson, Texas|Richardson]] (including the [[Collin County, Texas|Collin County]] portion) and the northern halves of [[Garland, Texas|Garland]] and [[Rowlett, Texas|Rowlett]], as well as the [[Far North Dallas|far north side]] of [[Dallas]] itself. The district then stretches eastward and crosses [[Lake Ray Hubbard]] to take in suburban [[Rockwall County, Texas|Rockwall County]] and the [[Lake Tawakoni]] area, traveling all the way east to such northern Tyler exurbs as [[Mineola, Texas|Mineola]] and [[Gilmer, Texas|Gilmer]].

Prior to redistricting, the incumbent was Democrat [[Julie Johnson (politician)|Julie Johnson]]; however, Johnson was drawn out of the 32nd district and into the 24th, ultimately deciding to seek reelection in the 33rd district (see below) and leaving this district as an open seat.<ref name="HPVT" /> In 2024, the new district gave 57.7% of the vote to Donald Trump and 55.2% to Ted Cruz, with Democrats only considered competitive in the Dallas County and Richardson portion of the otherwise heavily Republican district.

==District 33==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 33rd congressional district election

{{see also|Texas's 33rd congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 33rd congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 33rd congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 33rd congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 33rd district, previously encompassing mostly Hispanic parts of the [[Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex]] including [[Downtown Fort Worth]], western [[Dallas, Texas|Dallas]], and parts of [[Grand Prairie, Texas|Grand Prairie]], [[Irving, Texas|Irving]], [[Carrollton, Texas|Carrollton]], and [[Farmers Branch, Texas|Farmers Branch]], has since been redrawn to be exclusively within Dallas County. Prior to redistricting, the incumbent was Fort Worth-based Democrat [[Marc Veasey]]. However, Veasey was drawn out of the 33rd district and into the 25th, and ultimately chose to pursue a short-lived bid for Tarrant County judge before abandoning that bid. The new incumbent is Democrat [[Jasmine Crockett]], who was elected with 84.9% of the vote in 2024, in her previous District 30; however, Crockett chose to pursue a run for the United States Senate seat currently held by John Cornyn.<ref name="HPVT"/>

Former congressman and 2024 U.S. Senate nominee [[Colin Allred]] (who lost in the general election that year to [[Ted Cruz]]) and current 32nd District incumbent [[Julie Johnson (politician)|Julie Johnson]] ran for the Democratic nomination to represent this district, which is centered in [[Downtown Dallas|Downtown]] and [[Uptown, Dallas|Uptown Dallas]] and also extends in four separate directions - northwest to [[Dallas Love Field|Love Field]] and a northern section of [[Irving, Texas|Irving]] centered on the Valley Ranch area, northeast to the [[Swiss Avenue Historic District|Swiss Avenue]] and [[Texas State Highway Loop 12|Buckner Boulevard]] (east of [[White Rock Lake]]) corridors in [[east Dallas]], southeast to southeast Dallas (including [[Pleasant Grove, Dallas|Pleasant Grove]]) and [[Balch Springs, Texas|Balch Springs]], and southwest to [[West Dallas]] as well as [[Cockrell Hill, Texas|Cockrell Hill]] and central [[Grand Prairie, Texas|Grand Prairie]]. The new district gave 65.2% of the vote to Kamala Harris and 68.7% to Allred in 2024, and is over 50 percent Hispanic. Allred defeated Johnson in the runoff with 55% of the vote.

==District 34==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 34th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 34
| previous_year = 2024

| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Vicente Gonzalez, official portrait, 118th Congress (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Vicente Gonzalez (American politician)|Vicente Gonzalez]]
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Eric Flores
| party2 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 =

| before_election = [[Vicente Gonzalez (American politician)|Vicente Gonzalez]]

{{See also|Texas's 34th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 34th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 34th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 34th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 34th district stretches from [[Brownsville, Texas|Brownsville]] in the [[Rio Grande Valley (Texas)|Rio Grande Valley]], northward along the [[Gulf Coast of the United States|Gulf Coast]] to most of [[Corpus Christi, Texas|Corpus Christi]], covering all of [[Cameron County, Texas|Cameron]], [[Willacy County, Texas|Willacy]], [[Kenedy County, Texas|Kenedy]], [[Kleberg County, Texas|Kleberg]] and most of [[Nueces County, Texas|Nueces]] counties, essentially a recreation of the old 27th district from its establishment in 1982 until the 2010 election. The incumbent is Democrat [[Vicente Gonzalez (American politician)|Vicente Gonzalez]], who was re-elected with 51.3% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

Donald Trump won the district with 54.6% of the vote in 2024, having previously lost the district to Hillary Clinton and Joe Biden in 2016 and 2020, respectively, with Barack Obama winning the district twice in 2008 and 2012. Also, Ted Cruz won the district with a 49.7% plurality in 2024, even though the district gave 55% of the vote to Beto O'Rourke over Cruz in 2018.

The election was one of the most expensive congressional races in the 2026 elections, with over $30.8 million in spending.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Mutnick|first=Ally|date=October 6, 2026|title=The blockbuster House races|url=https://punchbowl.news/article/campaigns/blockbuster-house-races/|access-date=2026-10-15|website=Punchbowl News|language=en-US}}</ref>

==District 35==

{{see also|Texas's 35th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 35th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 35th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 35th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 35th district previously connected eastern [[San Antonio, Texas|San Antonio]] to southeastern [[Austin, Texas|Austin]], through the [[I-35]] corridor. However, with the new redistricting the 35th has been moved significantly to the south and east; it now covers much of south and northeast San Antonio, plus such suburbs as [[Live Oak, Texas|Live Oak]], [[Converse, Texas|Converse]] and [[Elmendorf, Texas|Elmendorf]] along with [[Guadalupe County, Texas|Guadalupe]] (including [[Seguin, Texas|Seguin]] and [[Schertz, Texas|Schertz]]), [[Wilson County, Texas|Wilson]] and [[Karnes County, Texas|Karnes]] counties.

Prior to redistricting, the incumbent was Democrat [[Greg Casar]]; however, Casar was drawn out of the 35th district and into the 37th.<ref name="HPVT" /> The new district remains majority Hispanic, albeit with over 53.7% of the voting age population being Hispanic, and 34.6% of the voting age population being White. [[Donald Trump]] won 54.6% of the vote in this district in 2024, having won by single-digit margins here in both 2016 and 2020, while [[Ted Cruz]] won 50.6% of the vote in this district in 2024.

====Campaign====
The Democratic runoff race for this district gained national attention in May 2026 after multiple comments made by candidate Maureen Galindo, who led the first round of the primary, were condemned by state and national Democrats for antisemitism. On social media, Galindo pledged to turn an [[List of immigrant detention sites in the United States|ICE Detention Center]] into a "prison for American Zionists", and later suggested on a radio broadcast that primary rival Johnny Garcia should be tried for [[Treason laws in the United States|treason]] due to his alleged support from Israel.<ref>{{cite news |last1=Karlis |first1=Michael |title=San Antonio congressional candidate Maureen Galindo wants rival tried for treason |url=https://www.sacurrent.com/news/san-antonio-congressional-candidate-maureen-galindo-wants-rival-tried-for-treason/ |access-date=20 May 2026 |work=San Antonio Current |date=15 May 2026}}</ref><ref name="Kamisar">{{cite news |last1=Kamisar |first1=Ben |title=House Democratic leaders condemn Texas candidate for antisemitic comments |url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/2026-election/house-democratic-texas-candidate-maureen-galindo-antisemitic-comments-rcna346022 |access-date=20 May 2026 |work=[[NBC News]] |date=19 May 2026}}</ref>

Following the remarks, John Lira, a former primary opponent who had previously endorsed Galindo, rescinded his endorsement.<ref name="JTA150526">{{cite news |last1=Lapin |first1=Andrew |title=Talarico won't campaign with Democratic House candidate who wants to open 'a prison for American Zionists' |url=https://www.jta.org/2026/05/15/politics/talarico-wont-campaign-with-democratic-house-candidate-who-wants-to-open-a-prison-for-american-zionists |access-date=20 May 2026 |work=[[Jewish Telegraphic Agency]] |date=15 May 2026}}</ref> Prominent Democrats directly rebuked Galindo's candidacy or endorsed Garcia, including U.S. Representatives [[Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez]], [[Suzan DelBene]] and [[Hakeem Jeffries]], alongside Texas state representatives [[James Talarico]] and [[Gina Hinojosa]], who are the Democratic nominees in the concurrent [[2026 United States Senate election in Texas|Senate]] and the [[2026 Texas gubernatorial election|gubernatorial elections]] respectively.<ref name="Kamisar"/><ref name="JTA150526"/><ref name="MGACAD">{{Cite tweet |author=Gina Hinojosa |user=GinaHinojosaTX |number=2057285819354788008 |title=Maureen Galindo's antisemitic comments are disqualifying. Anyone who campaigns on this kind of hate has no business anywhere near public office. |date=21 May 2026}}</ref>

==District 36==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 36th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 36
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 36
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Brian Babin 115th Congress 2 (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Brian Babin]]
| party1 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Rhonda Hart
| party2 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size = 
| map_caption = 
| title = U.S. Representative
| before_election = [[Brian Babin]]
| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election = 
| after_party =

{{see also|Texas's 36th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 36th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 36th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 36th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 36th district encompasses parts of [[Southeast Texas]], including the Harris County side of the [[Clear Lake (region)|Clear Lake region]] of Houston. While largely unchanged from its previous iteration, the new iteration includes [[Lufkin, Texas|Lufkin]] and the surrounding Piney Woods region as well as [[Silsbee, Texas|Silsbee]], [[Jasper, Texas|Jasper]] and most of [[Beaumont, Texas|Beaumont]], and also extends to almost all of [[Chambers County, Texas|Chambers County]] (including [[Mont Belvieu, Texas|Mont Belvieu]]) east of Houston) before extending into southeast [[Houston]] (including [[William P. Hobby Airport|Hobby Airport]] and [[Ellington Field Joint Reserve Base|Ellington Field]], as well as [[Glenbrook Valley, Houston|Glenbrook Valley]] and the aforementioned [[Clear Lake City (Greater Houston)|Clear Lake City]] development) along with the southeast [[Harris County, Texas|Harris County]] communities of [[Seabrook, Texas|Seabrook]], [[Webster, Texas|Webster]] and Harris County's portion of [[Friendswood, Texas|Friendswood]], and a small sliver of northern Brazoria County centered on most of [[Pearland, Texas|Pearland]].

The incumbent is Republican [[Brian Babin]], who was re-elected with 69.4% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT" /> Donald Trump won 61.8% of the vote in the new district in 2024, as did Ted Cruz with 59.1% of the vote.

==District 37==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 Texas's 37th congressional district election
| country = Texas
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 37
| previous_year = 2024
| next_election = 2028 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas#District 37
| next_year = 2028
| image1 = Greg Casar, official portrait, 118th Congress (cropped).jpg
| nominee1 = [[Greg Casar]]
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)
| popular_vote1 = 
| percentage1 = 
| image2 = 3x4.svg
| nominee2 = Lauren Peña
| party2 = Republican Party (United States)
| popular_vote2 = 
| percentage2 = 
| map_image = 
| map_size = 
| map_caption = 
| title = U.S. Representative
| before_election = [[Greg Casar]] ([[Democratic Party (United States)|Democratic]])<br />[[Lloyd Doggett]] ([[Democratic Party (United States)|Democratic]])
| after_election = 
| after_party = 
}}
{{see also|Texas's 37th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 37th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 37th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 37th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 37th district is based in all but the westernmost parts of [[Austin, Texas|Austin]], with virtually all of the precincts of the exclusively [[Travis County, Texas|Travis County]]-based district favoring Democrats to varying degrees; the new district has a White plurality with a 34% Hispanic voting age population. The incumbent is Democrat [[Lloyd Doggett]], who was re-elected with 75.9% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> On August 21, 2025, Doggett announced that he would not seek re-election due to mid-decade redistricting, and fellow Democratic Rep. [[Greg Casar]] being moved into the 37th district.<ref name="auto">{{cite web|url=https://www.texastribune.org/2025/08/21/lloyd-doggett-congress-retire-redistricting-greg-casar-austin/|title=Doggett won't seek reelection to Congress if new district is upheld by courts, paving way for Casar|last=Birenbaum|first=Gabby|date=August 21, 2025|access-date=August 21, 2025|website=[[Texas Tribune]]}}</ref> On August 25, 2025, Casar announced his bid for re-election from this district.<ref name="CasarHill"/> Kamala Harris won 76.8% of the vote in the new 37th District, which also gave 79.2% of the vote to Colin Allred; in both cases, the highest of any district amongst the state's new congressional districts.

==District 38==

{{see also|Texas's 38th congressional district}}
[[File:Texas's 38th congressional district (since 2027) (new version).svg|thumb|270px|Texas's 38th congressional district boundary from the 2026 elections<br />{{maplink|from=Texas's 38th congressional district (2027–).map|text=Interactive map version}}]]
The new 38th district, much like its original iteration first used in the 2022 election (and historically the base of the original Houston-based iteration of the 7th district from 1966 until the 2022 redistricting), is based in west [[Houston]] and northwest [[Harris County, Texas|Harris County]], including all or parts of the west Houston neighborhoods of [[River Oaks, Houston|River Oaks]], [[Tanglewood, Houston|Tanglewood]], [[Memorial City, Houston|Memorial City]], [[Spring Branch, Houston|Spring Branch]] and the [[Houston Energy Corridor|Energy Corridor]], as well as the communities of [[Jersey Village, Texas|Jersey Village]], [[Copperfield, Texas|Copperfield]], [[Cypress, Texas|Cypress]], [[Champion Forest, Texas|Champion Forest]], [[Klein, Texas|Klein]] and [[Tomball, Texas|Tomball]] in northwest Harris County.

The incumbent is Republican [[Wesley Hunt]], who was re-elected with 62.9% of the vote in 2024 and ran for the Republican nomination for U.S. Senate in 2026.<ref name="HPVT" /> Donald Trump won the district in 2024 with 59.5% of the vote, as did Ted Cruz (who resides in the district) with 56.6% of the vote.
